 2026:JHHC:32695
144
C.A.V. ON: 12/10/2026 PRONOUNCED ON:06/11/2026
1. Heard learned counsel for the parties.
2. Since, common issues are involved in the writ petitions,
they are heard together and are being disposed of by a common
order.
3. The petitioners in these batch of writ petitions have
prayed that the Notification dated 18.08.2026, bearing No.
06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of 2026, cancelling the recruitment
pursuant to Advertisement No. 10 of 2023 be quashed.
Background Facts:
4. The Jharkhand Staff Selection Commission (JSSC) issued
Advertisement No. 10 of 2023 for JGGLCCE-2023 (Regular),
covering the posts of Assistant Section Officer, Junior
Secretariat Assistant, Labour Enforcement Officer, Planning
Assistant, Block Welfare Officer, Block Supply Officer and
Circle Inspector-cum-Kanoongo. The examination was initially
scheduled for 28.01.2024 and 04.02.2024 was cancelled
following reports of a paper leak. The rescheduled examination
was conducted on 21.09.2024 and 22.09.2024.
5. Alleging malpractices in the conduct of the examination
two writ petitions, one a public interest litigation, being W.P.
(PIL) No. 5717 of 2024, Prakash Kumar & Ors., and another
W.P. (S) No. 1476 of 2025; the latter instituted by 1011
number of unsuccessful candidates came to be filed.

 2026:JHHC:32695
145
6. W.P. (PIL) No. 5717 of 2024 was preferred for the following
reliefs: -
(i) for conducting proper investigation/enquiry through a
court monitored Special Investigation Team (SIT) or
through an independent central agency into the serious
incident of paper leak having repeatedly taken place in
the examination of Jharkhand General Graduate Level
Combined Competitive Examination i.e. JGGLCCE-2023
(Regular) (hereinafter to be referred as “the said
examination”) held on 21/22.09.2024 conducted by the
Jharkhand Staff Selection Commission (JSSC) for
recruitment of 2021 persons on various posts which has
resulted in great turmoil and resentment amongst about
more than 3 lakh candidates who have appeared in the
said examination but have got prejudiced due to paper
leak.
(ii) for issuance of direction upon the respondents not to
publish the final result of the said examination as well
as not to issue offers of appointment unless and until
the investigation/enquiry into the matter of paper leak is
completed.
(iii) for commanding upon the respondents to frame and
implement a full proof mechanism so as to ensure
fairness in the competitive examinations in the State.
7. W.P.(S) No. 1476 of 2025 was preferred for the following
reliefs: -
(i) for quashing the Advertisement Nos. 10/2023 &
11/2023 floated by the JSSC to conduct Jharkhand
General Graduate Level Combined Competitive
Examination for the reason that the integrity of the said
examination has been compromised as the questions
related to the said examination were leaked much
before the commencement of the said examination.
(ii) for quashing the Notification No. 28, dated
04.12.2024 issued by the JSSC whereby the roll
numbers of the successful candidates have been
published and they have been called to appear for
documents verification.
(iii) for issuance of an appropriate direction to get the
entire recruitment process investigated by the Central

 2026:JHHC:32695
146
Bureau of Investigation (C.B.I.) which has been initiated
pursuant to the publication of Advertisement Nos.
10/2023 & 11/2023 floated by the JSSC.
8. The Division Bench vide an order dated 17.12.2024
directed registration of First Information Report and in
compliance thereof, CID Ranchi PS Case No. 01 of 2025 dated
01.01.2025 and CID Ranchi PS Case No. 02 of 2025 dated
03.01.2025 were registered for investigating offences under
sections 316(2)/ 318(2)/318(4)/61(2) of the BNS and sections
12(2) / 12(3) of the Jharkhand Competitive Examinations
(Measures for Control and Prevention of Unfair Means in
Recruitment) Act, 2023.
9. Both the writ petitions were heard together and decided
by a common judgment and order dated 03.12.2025 by a
Division Bench of this Court, whereby, inter alia, the following
directions were issued: -
“153. In view of the aforesaid discussions, we deem it appropriate
to pass the following directions: -
i. The status quo granted by this Court vide order dated 17.12.2024
passed in W.P.(PIL) No. 5717 of 2024 is hereby vacated.
ii. The respondent-JSSC is directed to publish the final result of the
said examination and the State Government is further directed to
appoint the successful candidates on the respective posts.
iii. The SIT is directed to complete the investigation of the case within
six months from the date of passing of the present judgement.
iv. Out of 28 candidates who were allegedly taken to Nepal for
mugging up the questions/answers, 10 were called for document
verification and as such the final result of the said 10 candidates
will be kept in abeyance till the investigation of SIT is completed as
well as their selection will be subject to the final outcome of the
investigation.

 2026:JHHC:32695
147
v. If in the investigation of SIT, some more successful candidates are
found involved in malpractice, the State shall take appropriate
action against the said candidates.
10. JSSC published the final result on 08.12.2025.
11. The above judgement dated 03.12.2025 was challenged
before the Hon’ble Supreme Court of India in S.L.P. (C) Nos.
38545–38546 of 2025 [Prakash Kumar Etc. v. State of
Jharkhand & Ors.] and by order dated 05.01.2026, the
Hon’ble Apex Court declined to interfere, while expressly
directing that all appointment letters issued or to be issued
remain subject to the final outcome of all criminal
prosecutions.
12. The respective petitioners received appointment letters
in December 2025-January 2026 and have since served on
their respective posts.
13. The investigation concerning the present examination is
still pending under F.I.R. No. 01 of 2025 and remains
incomplete.
14. Following protests staged at the end of July 2026 in
Ranchi, the State issued the impugned notification and
cancelled the present recruitment along with other
examinations. Basis such protest, another FIR being CID P.S.
Case No. 16 of 2026, dated 22.07.2026, concerned alleged
irregularities in the 14th Civil Services Examination

 2026:JHHC:32695
148
conducted by JPSC was registered and following that the
impugned decision to cancel altogether 22 examinations was
taken vide the impugned Notification dated 18.08.2026,
bearing No. 06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of 2026.
Submissions on Behalf of the Petitioners
15. The Petitioners assailed the impugned Notification on
following grounds:
(i) Impugned Decision is in the teeth of
judgment of Prakash Kumar (Supra)
16. The issue whether this examination should be cancelled
or preserved, with action against unidentifiable tainted
candidates, was directly considered in Prakash Kumar
(Supra). The State, being a party to those proceedings, was
bound to give effect to the directions for investigation and
candidate-specific action. It could not reopen the adjudicated
issue through executive action on substantially the same
allegations, without identifying fresh material warranting a
different course.
17. Reliance is placed on Hope Plantations Ltd. v. Taluk
Land Board, Peermade, (1999) 5 SCC 590, paragraphs 26
and 31, concerning finality, res judicata and issue of
estoppel, including their application to administrative
proceedings. The petitioners submit that the impugned action
undermines the binding framework of the earlier judgment

 2026:JHHC:32695
149
and the subsequent order of the Hon’ble Supreme Court.
18. The condition making appointments subject to the final
outcome of criminal prosecutions must be given effect
according to its terms. The petitioners seek protection of their
appointments pending that outcome and do not claim
immunity from investigation or lawful action against persons
found involved in malpractice. Reliance is also placed on
Pradeep Kumar Sonthalia v. Dhiraj Prasad Sahu, (2021)
6 SCC 523, paragraphs 34–36, for the presumption of
innocence and submit that allegations leading to
investigation and / or submission of chargesheet ipso facto
do not prove the culpability of the accused – candidate.
Neither a FIR, nor a Chargesheet, can be considered as legal
evidence and hence not material which can form the basis of
such en masse cancellation.
(II) Absence of a Foundation for Wholesale
Cancellation
19. The petitioners submit that allegations relating to a
different Commission and examination cannot, without a
demonstrated nexus, justify cancellation of this recruitment.
20. It has been submitted that the applicable test is whether
the material establishes a systemic breach, whether that
breach compromises the integrity of the entire examination,
and whether tainted candidates can be segregated from

 2026:JHHC:32695
150
untainted candidates. These considerations, discussed in
Vanshika Yadav v. Union of India, (2024) 9 SCC 743,
paragraphs 67, 68, 86 and 87, require an examination of
substantiated allegations and a proportionate response,
rather than cancellation founded on suspicion.
21. The petitioners submit that neither the impugned
notification, nor the counter affidavit discloses material
concerning this examination sufficient to establish pervasive
malpractice or the impossibility of segregation. No disclosed
report recommends cancellation of the examination as a
whole. The investigation remains pending; the six-month
period stipulated by the Division Bench expired on
03.06.2026 without a final report.
22. The letter as contained in Memo No. 936 dated
17.08.2026, placed as Annexure R/1 to the rejoinder in W.P.
(S) No. 6521 of 2026, is cited to demonstrate the inadequacy
of the material accompanying the Cabinet note. The
petitioners contend that public protests and allegations
relating to other examinations supplied the occasion for the
decision, but not an examination-specific evidentiary
foundation for it.
23. The narrative concerning BINSYS, ICN, WEBEL, ITI and
TDPL does not, according to the petitioners, identify their role
in the examination held on 21–22.09.2024 or establish a

 2026:JHHC:32695
151
connection with its question papers or results. Association
between vendors or individuals cannot substitute for material
connecting the alleged wrongdoing to Advertisement No. 10 of
2023. The petitioners further rely on the earlier DSP/SP
affidavits and the Division Bench’s finding that no leak from
an examination centre or during transit had been
established.
24. The allegations regarding candidates being taken to
Nepal as a part of the conspiracy and other allegations were
already before the Division Bench and have been considered
and acted upon. As recorded in the earlier judgment, the
candidates were allegedly given 123 questions to memorize, of
which 66 appeared, and only 10 of the 28 candidates were
selected. Paragraph 137(xii) is relied upon concerning the
absence of an unusual spike in the results of the centres
complained of. General references to malpractice in Nepal,
Bihar, West Bengal, Bokaro and Dhanbad do not, without
further particulars, establish that the entire examination was
compromised.
25. The State’s references to 265 candidates under notice,
as against 2,231 called for document verification, and to
arrests of selected candidates indicate that individual
suspects are being identified. The petitioners contend that
only three selected candidates had been arrested when the

 2026:JHHC:32695
152
notification was issued, one in the JPSC-related matter, and
that several arrests relied upon occurred thereafter.
Subsequent developments do not, by themselves, establish
that the requisite foundation existed on 18.08.2026. The
State must explain, on relevant material, why segregation
cannot be undertaken.
(III) Natural Justice and Proportionality
26. The petitioners are appointed public servants, not
merely candidates awaiting selection. Cancellation of the
recruitment has the substantive effect of terminating their
services. The State cannot avoid the applicable service rules
framed under Article 309, or the protection guaranteed by
Article 311, merely by describing its action as cancellation of
an examination. If the appointments are asserted to be void,
the foundational facts establishing that the process was a
nullity must first be demonstrated on relevant material.
27. The petitioners do not insist on separate hearings for
every appointee irrespective of the circumstances. They
submit, however, that the State was required to record its
reasons, justify any dispensation with a hearing, and
consider segregation. Reliance on a sealed-cover report does
not answer the challenge to the absence of disclosed reasons
and an adequate foundation for the impugned decision.
28. The petitioners also question the State’s authority to

 2026:JHHC:32695
153
cancel the recruitment process conducted by JSSC after
accepting its recommendations and issuing appointments.
The submission concerning the respective functions of the
State and the Commission under Articles 166 and 320, and
the applicable service framework, is pressed as an
independent objection to the competence and legality of the
impugned action. It was contended that the decision to cancel
the recruitment process pursuant to the Advertisement
issued by JSSC could only have been taken by the JSSC and
not the State Government.
29. The action is assailed as arbitrary and disproportionate
and as such violative of Articles 14, 16 and 21 of the
Constitution of India. Advertisement No. 10 of 2023
concerned 2017 posts and Advertisement No. 11 of 2023
concerned 8 backlog posts; both were examined and resulted
together, yet the latter is not mentioned in the cancellation.
The petitioners submit that this unexplained distinction
further indicates an absence of examination-specific
consideration.
30. The consequences for untainted appointees are
substantial: approximately 1932 serving appointees are
affected, and around 600 candidates are stated to have
resigned from earlier Government employment after the
judicial directions. The long interval without a successful

 2026:JHHC:32695
154
recruitment for these posts and the age profile of the
candidates compound the prejudice. These circumstances do
not protect fraudulent appointments, but require
consideration of less injurious measures before wholesale
cancellation.
(IV) Authorities Supporting Segregation and a
Proportionate Response
31. In Inderpreet Singh Kahlon v. State of Punjab,
(2006) 11 SCC 356, paragraphs 41, 44, 46, 50, 51, 58 and
69, the Court emphasised sufficient material gathered
through a fair investigation, the distinction between proven
mass cheating and allegations affecting serving civil servants,
and the obligation to endeavour to segregate tainted
candidates. Suspicion was held not to substitute for proof. In
Joginder Pal v. State of Punjab, (2014) 6 SCC 644,
paragraphs 26, 27, 32, 40 and 43, the protection of
candidates found untainted following the segregation exercise
was reiterated. These authorities support the petitioners’
objection to premature collective termination.
32. In Union of India v. Rajesh P.U. Puthuvalnikathu,
(2003) 7 SCC 285, paragraph 6, cancellation of the entire
selection was disapproved where scrutiny identified
irregularities affecting 31 candidates and no pervasive
infirmity justified denying appointment to the others.

 2026:JHHC:32695
155
Chairman, All India Railway Recruitment Board v. K.
Shyam Kumar, (2010) 6 SCC 614, paragraphs 16, 19, 36
and 37, concerned a retest supported by vigilance material
and explains the requirements of reasonableness and
proportionality. Anamica Mishra v. U.P. Public Service
Commission, 1990 Supp SCC 692, is similarly relied upon
for a remedy confined to the defective interview stage rather
than cancellation of the written examination.
33. Sachin Kumar v. Delhi Subordinate Service
Selection Board, (2021) 4 SCC 631, paragraph 35,
distinguishes systemic irregularities warranting cancellation
from identifiable individual wrongdoing. Although
cancellation was upheld on the facts of that case, the
principle remains that untainted candidates should not bear
the consequences of others’ misconduct where segregation is
possible. Vanshika Yadav, particularly paragraphs 39, 67,
68, 86 and 87, show that the Hon’ble Supreme Court
declined to order a fresh examination where widespread
compromise was not established and beneficiaries of
malpractice could be identified.
34. In Onkar Lal Bajaj v. Union of India, (2003) 2 SCC
673, paragraphs 36, 43, 45 and 56, the Court emphasised
justice, equity and fair play, and disapproved indiscriminate
cancellation prompted by controversy without examination of

 2026:JHHC:32695
156
relevant facts. The petitioners invoke that reasoning against a
collective response to protests which, on their case,
substitutes public pressure for an objective assessment of
this recruitment.
35. For the foregoing reasons, the petitioners respectfully
pray that the Notification dated 18.08.2026, bearing No.
06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of 2026, be quashed and set aside
insofar as it cancels the recruitment pursuant to
Advertisement No. 10 of 2023.
36. The petitioners further pray that their appointments and
continuance in service be protected pending the final
outcome of all criminal prosecutions, in accordance with the
order dated 05.01.2026 in S.L.P. (C) Nos. 38545–38546 of
2025.
37. It has been categorically submitted that the petitioners
undertake to cooperate with the investigation. The relief
sought does not impede investigation, identification of
beneficiaries of malpractice, or lawful action consistent with
the earlier judicial directions.
Submissions on Behalf of the respondents
38. The Respondent-State defends the cancellation of the
Jharkhand Combined Civil Services Examination-2023,
arguing that systemic fraud compromised the entire
recruitment process.

 2026:JHHC:32695
157
39. The Respondents seek to rely on CID enquiries, an FIR,
arrests of Commission officials and agency directors, alleged
OMR tampering, digital manipulation, and destruction of
evidence.
40. The State argues that honest candidates cannot reliably
be separated from beneficiaries because both original records
and digital audit trails were compromised.
41. The respondents further contend that mass cancellation
requires sufficient evidence of systemic irregularity—not
criminal proof beyond reasonable doubt—and may proceed
without individual hearings and hence the impugned
decision, which is based on sufficient reasonable materials,
needs no interference.
42. Rebutting the contention of the petitioners’ that
cancellation is premature because the investigation is
pending, the respondents have submitted that the executive
is not required to defer action until criminal convictions are
secured. Reference was made to the decision of the Supreme
Court in State of T.N. v. A. Kalaimani, (2021) 16 SCC 217
wherein the Hon’ble Supreme Court upheld cancellation
while the investigation was actively underway and arrests
were ongoing.
43. In so far as Article 311 argument of the petitioners is
concerned, the State characterizes the action as annulment of

 2026:JHHC:32695
158
a defective selection process, rather than punishment for
individual misconduct and hence contends that the
constitutional protection would not be available.
44. It was submitted that under the terms of Advertisement
No. 01 of 2024, candidature remains strictly provisional at all
stages. Selection confers no vested or indefeasible right to
appointment unless the Government is satisfied as to
suitability.
45. Further it was urged that the Jharkhand Competitive
Examinations (Measures for Prevention and Prohibition of
Unfair Means in Recruitment) Act, 2023 treats organized
interference with public examinations as a severe statutory
crime and the pending criminal prosecution invokes this
statute.
46. On the scope of judicial review, it has been contended
that courts should examine the decision’s supporting
material and rationality, rather than substitute their own
preferred remedy.
47. The Respondents have urged this Court to consider
sealed-envelope material apart from the materials brought on
record.
48. Two tables summarizing the supporting precedents and
distinguishing the cases relied upon by the petitioners as
prepared by the respondents is as follows:

49. Lastly, it was submitted that the Division Bench in
Prakash Kumar v. State of Jharkhand, W.P.(PIL) No.
5717 of 2024, declined to quash the examination in a PIL
because the material on record did not show official

 2026:JHHC:32695
164
complicity or question paper leaks but expressly permitted
the ongoing criminal investigation to continue and reserved
the State's liberty to proceed against any further beneficiaries
discovered during the investigation. Subsequent to that
judgment, further complaints and CID investigation reports
disclosed deeper institutional contamination, leading to the
Cabinet decision dated 17.08.2026. Hence, it was contended,
the earlier judgment of the Division Bench and the order of
the Hon’ble Supreme Court of India are of no help to the
petitioner and consequently the principles of res judicata and
estoppel in any form or substance do not come to the rescue
of the petitioners.
50. The counsel for the applicants who had filed
intervention application were permitted to address this Court,
though their applications have not been allowed. Similar
arguments to that of the State Government were advanced.
Hence submission recorded on behalf of the State
Government will also be treated to be their submissions.
51. In the light of the afore-stated facts and law the
respondents have urged to dismiss the petition.
52. Analysis and Findings

 2026:JHHC:32695
165
53. On perusal of the materials on record, the admitted
facts that emerge are that the petitioners of the batch of writ
petitions were appointed pursuant to the recruitment process
initiated pursuant to Advertisement 10 of 2023 and the said
process has been cancelled by the impugned Notification
dated 18.08.2026, bearing No. 06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of
2026. On allegations of malpractice in the recruitment
process and alleging that the process was tainted, an FIR was
registered on the directions of this Court, and the
investigation is still underway. Earlier a Division Bench of
this Court refused to cancel the examination on the
allegations of paper leak and other malpractices, which are
more or less identical to what has been purportedly
considered by the State Government while passing the
impugned order. The Division Bench further directed the
investigation to be carried out by a SIT within 6 months. The
Hon’ble Supreme Court while dismissing Special Leave
Petition against the judgment and order of the High Court
directed that the appointment of the petitioners shall be
subject to the outcome of the final prosecution.
54. Having regard to the judgment and order passed in
Prakash Kumar v. State of Jharkhand, W.P.(PIL) No.
5717 of 2024 and the order of the Hon’ble Supreme Court in
S.L.P. (C) Nos. 38545–38546 of 2025, the impugned order

 2026:JHHC:32695
166
has the effect to overreaching the same. Once the High Court
and the Supreme Court had the occasion to deal with the
issue, which is directly and substantially the same as the
present one, the respondents only by claiming that some
further material has been collected and further arrests had
been made cannot undo the judgment and order of
Constitutional Courts. The direction issued by the Division
Bench permitting the investigation to be carried out further
and complete the same within a time frame and also that of
the Hon’ble Supreme Court making the appointments of
these petitioners subject to the final outcome of the
prosecution clearly indicates that the Hon’ble Courts were
cognizant of the fact that further materials may or may not
come against the candidates and despite that did not reserve
any liberty with the State of Jharkhand to take a decision to
cancel the concluded recruitment / examination process or
terminate the services of all or any of the petitioners before
the prosecution is complete.
55. In view of the above, there can be no hesitation to hold
that State of Jharkhand has breached the orders passed by
the Hon’ble Division Bench and the Hon’ble Supreme Court of
India and the impugned decision as contained in Notification
dated 18.08.2026, bearing No. 06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of

 2026:JHHC:32695
167
2026 runs completely contrary to the said judgment and
order and seeks to overreach the same.
56. An act of the executive cannot be contrary to a judicial
order. Likewise, judicial decisions, inter alia, are aimed to
give quietus to a dispute and bring about certainty. Legal
certainty is a Rule of Law. Post final determination of the
issues by the Division Bench and the Supreme Court, it is
not permissible for the State Government to reopen the same
issue that too in a manner in which it has been done by the
impugned notification.
57. Records reveal that the State Government acting
through its cabinet took the impugned decision, interalia,
because of the protests. Reference was also made to the
investigation. However, the cabinet note does not disclose
what were the exact materials placed before it to arrive at the
decision to cancel not only the present examination but 21
other examinations.
58. It is true that a writ court is not supposed to look into
the sufficiency of the materials, but it is equally true that
reasonable materials must exist, which must be considered to
arrive at a conclusion and in absence thereof the decision is
liable to be struck down as perverse.

 2026:JHHC:32695
168
59. The law with respect to the powers of the Government to
cancel a recruitment process is no more res integra. At the
same time, it is trite that a judgment is an authority only in
regard to its ratio which is required to be discerned; and a
decision cannot be regarded as an authority in regard to its
conclusion alone or even in relation to what could be deduced
therefrom. [ Suneja Towers (P) Ltd. v. Anita Merchant,
(2023) 9 SCC 194]. Keeping this proposition of law let us
examine the decisions on which the respondents have relied
upon to test their applicability to the facts of this case.
60. In Bihar School Examination Board v. Subhas
Chandra Sinha, (1970) 1 SCC 648, paragraphs 5 and 12,
concerned mass copying at a particular examination centre,
supported by answer-book comparison; it did not concern
termination of serving appointees. Madhyamic Shiksha
Mandal, M.P. v. Abhilash Shiksha Prasar Samiti, (1998)
9 SCC 236, paragraph 2, involved contemporaneous reports
of paper leakage, copying material and ineffective supervision,
making identification of innocent candidates extremely
difficult. The petitioners submit that comparable material is
absent here.
61. Gohil Vishvaraj Hanubhai v. State of Gujarat,
paragraphs 7, 8, 10 and 22 concerned a concrete OMR

 2026:JHHC:32695
169
anomalies, including 284 specially marked sheets and
unusual familial and address patterns in the merit list. State
of T.N. v. A. Kalaimani, (2021) 16 SCC 217, paragraph 14,
concerned an initial enquiry identifying 196 beneficiaries of
OMR manipulation before cancellation, followed by further
investigative material. Those decisions cannot be applied
mechanically to a case in which, according to the petitioners,
neither comparable OMR manipulation; nor systemic
compromise has been shown.
62. Nidhi Kaim v. State of Madhya Pradesh, (2016) 7
SCC 615, paragraphs 46 – 49, 60, 70, 84, and 136,
concerned a documented scheme affecting medical
admissions over successive academic sessions. Action was
taken against 634 identified beneficiaries. The identification
of the beneficiaries upon due investigation / concluded
enquiry forms the basis of this judgment rather than any
proposition that unrelated, untainted candidates must also
lose their appointments.
63. In State of West Bengal v. Baishakhi Bhattacharyya
(Chatterjee), (2025) 11 SCC 129, paragraphs 35, 37, 38, 39,
42 and 45, extensive material from CBI reports and the
Justice (Retd.) Bag Committee, including rank jumping, out-of-panel appointments and manipulation of OMR scores. The

 2026:JHHC:32695
170
Court’s conclusions rested on an in-depth factual enquiry
demonstrating deliberate compromise of the selection
process. There is no comparable foundation that has been
disclosed in the present case.
64. The distinction between the evidentiary standard
applicable to an administrative decision concerning systemic
malpractice and proof of individual guilt in a criminal
prosecution is beyond any quarrel. However, even the
reasonable-certainty or probability standard discussed in
Baishakhi Bhattacharyya (supra) requires sufficient
material obtained through a fair and thorough investigation.
Neither the pendency of an investigation nor general
allegations concerning several examinations dispense with
that requirement or the consideration of segregation.
65. The Supreme Court of India Chairman, All India
Railway Recruitment Board v. K. Shyam Kumar, (2010) 6
SCC 614, upheld the State's decision to order a fresh
examination as a valid bona fide executive choice by
observing that the decision cannot be displaced on judicial
review in the backdrop of an established case of paper leak
and after completion of a vigilance enquiry. In the present
case there is no in-depth enquiry concluded and a final report
prepared. The facts are hazy and sketchy. The State except

 2026:JHHC:32695
171
for making a bald assertion that segregation may not be
possible has not assigned any reasons or disclosed any
cogent material in support of such averment on affidavit.
66. In B. Ramanjini v. State of A.P., (2002) 5 SCC 533,
case of paper leak stood established in the District of
Anantapur and only in that District was the Examination
cancelled. This clearly reflects application of mind and is a
case where segregation was acted upon. Challenge was also
negated on account of delay in approaching the Tribunal of
first instance. The factual matrix in the present case is clearly
different.
67. The Supreme Court of India in Krishan Yadav v. State
of Haryana, (1994) 4 SCC 165, interfered with the
appointments after fraud was established after due enquiry
was conducted and concluded by the CBI. To apply the ratio
of this judgment the respondents ought to have firstly
satisfied that a case of institutional fraud was established by
materials and not on suspicion and it is only thereafter it was
held that subsequent appointment cannot protect a selection
born of institutional fraud.
68. Similarly, in Union of India v. O. Chakradhar, (2002)
3 SCC 146, the report furnished by the CBI was relied upon
before setting aside the appointments. The CBI concluded

 2026:JHHC:32695
172
that answer sheet packets were stealthily opened and
answers were filled up in the blank spaces left by the
examinees and a clear difference of handwriting was noticed
in many answer sheets. Similarly, interview marks were also
found to be tampered.
69. In Shankarsan Dash v. Union of India, (1991) 3 SCC
47, it was held that inclusion in a merit list or selection panel
confers no indefeasible constitutional right to appointment.
70. There cannot be any difficulty in accepting the above
proposition of law, However, in the present case the
petitioners have already been appointed and therefore a right
has been created and the same cannot be taken away without
following the due process. It may, in a given case, be possible
that prior notice or hearing be done away with, but such an
exception can only be made when facts supporting such
exercise are available on record.
71. In the present case as noted, the materials collected in
an incomplete investigation cannot be pressed into service to
do away with the principles of natural justice.
72. Further, the question whether segregation is possible or
the beneficiaries are identifiable can only be answered after
conclusion of the investigation and not before that. In any

 2026:JHHC:32695
173
event in the present case the judgment of Prakash Kumar
(supra) cannot be lost sight of and must be obeyed and hence
decision on the continuance of the services of these
petitioners would depend on the final outcome of the
prosecution and not at any stage prior thereto.
73. In the light of the above discussions and the settled legal
principles it is held that the Notification dated 18.08.2026,
bearing No. 06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of 2026, cancelling the
recruitment pursuant to Advertisement No. 10 of 2023, is
liable to be quashed and set aside for the following reasons:
i. There is no in-depth factual inquiry revealing
systematic irregularities that undermine the
integrity of the entire selection process. No
material has been collected through a concluded
investigation, forming the factual basis, which on
application of the probability test, would prove
with reasonable certainty that a systematic fraud
or malaise has been committed compromising the
entire selection process.
ii. It cannot be inferred at this stage that broad and
deep manipulation in the selection process is
proven, and the facts establish that the entire
selection process is vitiated with illegalities at a
large scale.
iii. The stage of segregation, if at all, is yet to come as
the investigation is underway and therefore the
State must if and when possible, should

 2026:JHHC:32695
174
undertake this exercise in consonance with
fairness and equity. In absence of such an
examination / exercise at the appropriate stage,
the decision to en masse cancel the subject
examination along with 21 others cannot be
sustained.
iv. The issues raised by the State Government cannot
be permitted to be reagitated so as to undo the
binding effect of the judgment of Prakash Kumar
(Supra). The judgment is valid and binding and
the State cannot be permitted to act contrary
thereto.
v. The State Government has also failed to establish
facts which can possibly justify the doing away
with the compliance of the Principles of Natural
Justice, such exemption being not automatic but
is in the nature of exception and hence must be
based on terra firma.
74. Accordingly, it is ordered that the Notification dated
18.08.2026, bearing No. 06/LO.S.A.-01-07/5408 of 2026,
cancelling the recruitment pursuant to Advertisement No. 10
of 2023 is quashed and set aside and the petitioners would
be deemed to be in service with continuity in service without
any break and they should not be denied of any service
benefits, be it monetary or otherwise, on account of the
impugned order.
75. However, the judgment dated 03.12.2025 of the Hon’ble
High Court in W.P. (PIL) No. 5717 of 2024, and W.P. (S) No.

76. Further, the investigation must be carried out to its
logical conclusion in a fair and free manner with a hope that
the process be expedited. The petitioners must cooperate with
the investigation. No observation made in this order will
prejudice the ongoing investigation and it is made clear that
the discussions made in this order are only for the purposes
of the present proceedings.
77. Having regards to the aforesaid discussions, the writ
petitions are allowed. Costs made easy. Pending I.As if any
also stands disposed of.
